## Exodus 14:4 (id: 11057)

* *know that I am the LORD:* This event was the climactic (final and most important) demonstration of the Lord’s character and power in the Exodus. Through it, God showed clearly who he is and what he can do.

    Rescue comes through God’s self\-revelation. This means that God saves people by making himself known.

    God does this by entering into human life and experience. He makes himself present and active among his people.

* *this is what the Israelites did:* In this part of the book, the Israelites were obedient to God’s commands ([12:35](https://ref.ly/Exod12:35), [50](https://ref.ly/Exod12:50)). Unfortunately, they did not continue this pattern after crossing the Red Sea.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`)

## Exodus 14:5–14 (id: 11058)

Pharaoh and his officials did not truly repent. Once the immediate fear faded, they focused on their own interests and they determined to recapture their slave labor.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:5-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Baal-zephon (ID: `place:Baal-zephon`); Pi-hahiroth (ID: `place:Pi-hahiroth`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Exodus 14:6–7 (id: 11059)

chariot: At this time in history, the Egyptian light chariot was the top weapon. Pulled by three horses, it was fast and easy to maneuver. Sometimes one person operated it, but some ancient pictures show a driver with a warrior. The mention of officers may suggest these two\-man teams. The world's strongest military power was gathering against the Hebrews.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:6-7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Exodus 14:9 (id: 11060)

Today, we do not know the exact place where these events happened (see study note on 14:2). However, the original narrator and the first people to hear this story did know the location.

We should not assume that the account is fictional just because we lack precise geographic details today. The story is presented as a real event that took place in a known setting.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:9
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 14:2 (ID: `11055@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Baal-zephon (ID: `place:Baal-zephon`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Pi-hahiroth (ID: `place:Pi-hahiroth`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Exodus 14:10–12 (id: 11048)

This complaint is the first of many that would follow during the next 40 years. Instead of trusting the God who had shown his great power, the Israelites turned against the one who had rescued them.

Their response shows a deeper problem. An unsurrendered heart (a person’s inner attitude, will, or decision\-making center) prefers safety over trust.

In simple terms, their attitude was this: “It is better to return to the security of slavery than to face the risk of trusting God.”

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:10-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Exodus 14:13–14 (id: 11049)

At least one person learned from the plagues and applied it to this crisis of faith. Moses did not know what God would do, but he made one of the great statements of faith in the Bible. Moses expressed his confidence in God. It was not the Lord who would fail, but the Egyptians.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:13-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

## Exodus 14:15–31 (id: 11050)

The escape through the Red Sea was the climactic moment of rescue. It was the highest point of God’s saving work in the exodus from Egypt.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:15-31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Believe In (ID: `keyterm:BelieveIn`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Wagon (ID: `realia:Wagon`)

## Exodus 14:17 (id: 11051)

*honor*: The Hebrew word translated as *honor* (*kabod*) suggests weightiness, importance, and reality. It describes something solid and true. It is not just brightness or outward beauty.

In this event, God showed that he is real and powerful. At the same time, he showed that the political, military, and material glory of one of the greatest human cultures was weak and temporary. It was only a thin covering that could not stand before him.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`)

## Exodus 14:19–25 (id: 11052)

The night before, the Hebrews were afraid and in a panic. Now the Egyptian army was confused and in disorder. Even though they were highly trained, they could not keep control.

They realized that they faced a power greater than themselves. They could not understand it, and they could not control it.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:19-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Egyptians (ID: `group:Egypt`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); An Angel (ID: `deity:Angel`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Wagon (ID: `realia:Wagon`)

## Exodus 14:22 (id: 11053)

As with the plagues, natural explanations for this event miss the main point. Some people suggest that a strong wind could push back shallow water. That can happen in nature.

However, a steady wind does not create dry ground with walls of water on both sides. The Bible describes something greater than an ordinary weather event.

The Lord rules over nature. He can act within it and use it as he chooses.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:22

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Exodus 14:25 (id: 11054)

* *He caused their chariot wheels to wobble:* The ancient versions differ on the translation of this phrase. Perhaps there was deep sand where the water had been, and this broke the light wheels of the chariots. In any case, the Egyptian charge through the sea failed, and they became terrified.
* *the LORD is fighting for them:* By this time, everyone in Egypt knew about the special relationship between the Lord and his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Wagon (ID: `realia:Wagon`)

## Exodus 14:31 (id: 11056)

* *the people feared the LORD and believed in Him*: Sadly, their faith did not last long (see [15:24](https://ref.ly/Exod15:24); [16:3](https://ref.ly/Exod16:3)).
* To be the Lord’s servant is a position of high honor (see [Isaiah 42:1–4](https://ref.ly/Isa42:1-Isa42:4); [Matthew 12:18](https://ref.ly/Matt12:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 14:31

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Believe In (ID: `keyterm:BelieveIn`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Exodus 15:1 (id: 11069)

* *Moses and the Israelites:* In this joyful moment, there was no division between them. They sang together as one people.
* *I will sing:* The first two verses use very personal words. They show that God relates to people in a personal way. He is not an impersonal force. He relates to us as one person relates to another.

    In the Bible, songs of thanks and praise usually state the reason for praise near the beginning (see [Psalm 95:1–3](https://ref.ly/Ps95:1-Ps95:3); [96:1–4](https://ref.ly/Ps96:1-Ps96:4)). Here, the reason is clear: the Lord has triumphed gloriously.

    During the dark night, the people trusted God. Now their trust was shown to be right. God had given them victory.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`)

## Exodus 15:1–5 (id: 11062)

God’s salvation (deliverance) and rescue had meaning for personal faith.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:1-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Red Sea (ID: `place:RedSea`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Cattails (ID: `flora:Cattails`)

## Exodus 15:1–18 (id: 11061)

Scholars believe this song of rescue to be one of the oldest preserved examples of the Hebrew language, attesting to its importance in Israel’s thought and faith. It is divided into three stanzas:

1. The first stanza celebrates the Lord's rescue of Moses and his people (using many first\-person pronouns, [15:1–5](https://ref.ly/Exod15:1-Exod15:5)).
2. The second stanza celebrates the great difference between the Lord and the Egyptians (verses [6–12](https://ref.ly/Exod15:6-Exod15:12)).
3. The third stanza considers what these events would mean for the future (verses [13–18](https://ref.ly/Exod15:13-Exod15:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:1-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Leader (ID: `keyterm:Leader`); Red Sea (ID: `place:RedSea`); Philistia (ID: `place:Philistine`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Pharaoh (ID: `person:Pharaoh.6`); Edom (ID: `place:Edom`); Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Terror (ID: `keyterm:Terror`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`); Cattails (ID: `flora:Cattails`); Horse (ID: `fauna:Horse`); Chariot (ID: `realia:Chariot`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Exodus 15:2 (id: 11076)

This statement is quoted in two other places, [Psalm 118:14](https://ref.ly/Ps118:14) and [Isaiah 12:2](https://ref.ly/Isa12:2). This shows its importance. The “God of your father”([Exodus 3:6](https://ref.ly/Exod3:6)) had become *my God*. The Israelites now knew God personally, not only as a historical memory.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

## Exodus 15:3 (id: 11077)

*The LORD is a warrior:* He will strongly defend his people. He is not an oppressor and does not fight for violence's sake. When his people are helpless against enemies, whether from outside or within the people, they can trust that Yahweh, "the One Who Is," is their defender ([Isaiah 59:15–19](https://ref.ly/Isa59:15-Isa59:19); [63:1–6](https://ref.ly/Isa63:1-Isa63:6); [Revelation 11:17–18](https://ref.ly/Rev11:17-Rev11:18)). For more on the name *Yahweh,* see study note on Exodus 3:15.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:3
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 3:15 (ID: `11277@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 15:6 (id: 11079)

*Your right hand:* This was usually the arm a warrior used to hold a sword.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 15:6–12 (id: 11078)

The song speaks to the Lord in a personal way. It calls him *You*. This makes a strong contrast between the Lord and *them*, the enemies ([15:7](https://ref.ly/Exod15:7)). The enemies had their own plans and desires, but their plans failed ([15:9](https://ref.ly/Exod15:9),[10](https://ref.ly/Exod15:10)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:6-12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Strength (ID: `keyterm:Strength`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Exodus 15:8 (id: 11080)

* *nostrils:* In other contexts, this word may be translated as *anger*. The hot breath from God's nose is an image of God's anger.
* *the blast:* The Hebrew word translated as *breath* (*ruakh*) is also translated as *wind* in [14:21](https://ref.ly/Exod14:21) and as *Spirit* in [Genesis 1:2](https://ref.ly/Gen1:2). Nature is not God, but God works everywhere in nature.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:8


## Exodus 15:9–10 (id: 11081)

Compared to the Creator’s plans, all the plans of even the most powerful humans are nothing (see [Psalm 2:2–5](https://ref.ly/Ps2:2-Ps2:5)). They are like dust that can be blown away with one puff of his breath (see [Isaiah 40:15–17](https://ref.ly/Isa40:15-Isa40:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:9-10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Dispossess (ID: `keyterm:Dispossess`); Mighty (ID: `keyterm:Mighty`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Exodus 15:11 (id: 11063)

The gods of the pagan nations are not in the same category as the Lord. They do not deserve to be called holy (see study note on 3:5).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:11
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 3:5 (ID: `11285@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 15:13 (id: 11065)

* Because of God's constant love and power, we can trust that he will keep his promises. *Loving devotion* is a translation of the Hebrew word *khesed*, which refers to the undeserved kindness and loyalty from a superior to an inferior. This is the most common way to describe God's character in the Old Testament (see [Psalm 136](https://ref.ly/Ps136:1-Ps136:26)).

    If God had this character but lacked the power to fulfill his good intentions for his people, his character would not benefit us. The good news is that he has both a loving character and all power.

* *holy dwelling:* God has chosen his people and gathered them to live among them (see also [Exodus 15:17](https://ref.ly/Exod15:17)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:13

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`)

## Exodus 15:13–18 (id: 11064)

The people God has redeemed have confidence for the future. Because of what the Lord had done in rescuing Israel from his enemies, there was no doubt that he would be able to carry through on his promise to take them safely into the promised land.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:13-18

## Exodus 15:14–15 (id: 11066)

Philistia, Edom*,* Moab*,* and Canaan are the four peoples that would be most affected by God's judgment when he gave their land to the people of Israel. News of what God did to Egypt was probably well\-known in that region (see Rahab’s report in [Joshua 2:9–11](https://ref.ly/Josh2:9-Josh2:11), where it uses the same imagery as in [Exodus 15:15](https://ref.ly/Exod15:15), "will melt away").

## Exodus 15:24 (id: 11073)

Instead of thinking about recent lessons in faith, the people lost hope and started to complain, as is often the case in difficult times. If complaining becomes a habit, it can lead to failure in the ultimate test, as it did for the Israelites (see [Numbers 14](https://ref.ly/Num14:1-Num14:45); [Psalm 95:7–11](https://ref.ly/Ps95:7-Ps95:11); [Hebrews 3:7–11](https://ref.ly/Heb3:7-Heb3:11)).

## Exodus 15:26 (id: 11075)

The link between obeying God’s commands and health is not random. God made the human body and cares about how his people live. In this covenant promise, God told Israel that obedience would bring protection from the diseases that affected Egypt

This statement was made when the people needed water. It may suggest that the Egyptians had been drinking polluted water, which could make people sick.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 15:26

## Exodus 16:3 (id: 11089)

*ate our fill of bread:* The Israelites had been oppressed slaves in Egypt. Their complaint did not match the reality of their suffering.

One danger of complaining is that it can blind people to the truth. It can make the past seem better than it really was.

Faith responds differently. Faith is thankful for what God has already done and trusts him for what he will do in the future.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Cooking Pot (ID: `realia:CookingPot`)

## Exodus 16:4–5 (id: 11090)

These were the Lord’s instructions for gathering the food he gave in the wilderness. He provided enough for each day. On the sixth day, he gave a double portion. This way, the people did not need to gather food on the Sabbath (see [16:21–30](https://ref.ly/Exod16:21-Exod16:30)). 

The Israelites kept the Sabbath even before it was written in the Ten Commandments.

Many people resist a way of life that requires daily trust in God. We prefer to store up supplies so that we feel independent. But God was teaching his people to live by faith. They had to trust him each day to provide what they needed (compare [Matthew 6:11](https://ref.ly/Matt6:11)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:4-5

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 16:7 (id: 11091)

The Hebrew word translated *glory* has a deeper meaning than the English word often suggests.

In Hebrew, the word points to weightiness, substance, and reality. It describes something solid and important, not something light or short\-lived.

In English, glory can sound bright or impressive but temporary. The Hebrew idea is stronger. It is closer to royal grandeur. It shows greatness, honor, and true authority.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:7

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`)

## Exodus 16:15 (id: 11083)

The Hebrew term *man hu* (meaning "What is it?") became the name for the miraculous food called manna (see [16:31](https://ref.ly/Exod16:31)). For 40 years, the people ate *what is it?* Jesus referred to himself as the fulfillment of the meaning of this miracle. He is the "true bread from heaven" that gives life ([John 6:32–35](https://ref.ly/John6:32-John6:35), [48](https://ref.ly/John6:48), [51](https://ref.ly/John6:51), [63](https://ref.ly/John6:63)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

The Sabbath was a day set apart for the Lord ([16:23](https://ref.ly/Exod16:23)). It was also a gift from the Lord to his people ([16:29](https://ref.ly/Exod16:29)). Rest and worship are not meant to be an obligation, but a privilege. However, because people often try to meet their needs in our own way, God commands rest and worship ([16:28](https://ref.ly/Exod16:28)).

## Exodus 16:35 (id: 11088)

*until they came to a land:* God’s care for us is normally demonstrated in ordinary ways, as it would usually be for Israel after they arrived in Canaan (see [Joshua 5:10–12](https://ref.ly/Josh5:10-Josh5:12)). That care is just as real as when it comes in extraordinary ways, as it did while Israel was in the wilderness.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 16:35

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Canaan (ID: `place:Canaan`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Exodus 17:1 (id: 11095)

*from place to place:* Because the group was large and included people of many ages, their travel was likely slow and difficult.

God may also have used this time to show his care for them. He provided for their needs in miraculous ways. He gave them food and water in the wilderness.

Before he brought them to Mount Sinai and offered them his covenant, he showed that he could be trusted. He cared for them even before he gave them his law.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 17:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Rephidim (ID: `place:Rephidim`); Wilderness of Sin (ID: `place:Sin`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Exodus 17:2 (id: 11096)

*test the LORD*: This is explained in [17:7](https://ref.ly/Exod17:7). The people doubted that God was with them. They questioned whether he cared for them. They demanded proof that he was present and would help them.

There is a kind of testing that comes from faith. A person may believe, yet still struggle with doubt (“I do believe; help my unbelief,” [Mark 9:24](https://ref.ly/Mark9:24)). This is a humble request for help.

But there is also a test that comes from unbelief (“so that we may see it and believe You? What will You do?” [John 6:30](https://ref.ly/John6:30)). They wanted proof before they would trust him.

A test based on doubt puts God on trial. It treats God as if he must prove himself to us. It places us in the position of judge and God in the position of the one being judged.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 17:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 17:8 (id: 11100)

Amalek was the grandson of Esau ([Genesis 36:11–12](https://ref.ly/Gen36:11-Gen36:12)). Amalek’s descendants were a nomadic people. This means they moved from place to place instead of living in one city.

They were loosely based in the land of Edom. This means Edom was their main area, even though they traveled often. They likely supported themselves by attacking and robbing nearby settled communities.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 17:8

## Exodus 17:9–13 (id: 11101)

This victory was a gift from God. The description in this passage makes that clear.

God’s blessing determined the outcome of the battle. Moses showed this by raising his hands. When his hands were lifted up, Israel prevailed.

This pattern appeared again and again when Israel entered the land of Canaan. When God blessed them, they succeeded.

But without God’s blessing, Israel could do nothing. They failed when they tried to fight on their own (see [Numbers 14:42–45](https://ref.ly/Num14:42-Num14:45); [Joshua 7:10–12](https://ref.ly/Josh7:10-Josh7:12)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 17:9-13

## Exodus 17:14–16 (id: 11092)

The Amalekites attacked Israel without cause. They attacked the people through whom God was bringing blessing to the world. Because of this, they brought God’s lasting anger on themselves.

God can stretch out his hand to bless people. He can also stretch out his hand to judge and punish. If people refuse his blessing, they experience his judgment instead (see [1 Samuel 15](https://ref.ly/1Sam15:1-1Sam15:35)).

## Exodus 18:13–27 (id: 11105)

Jethro’s wise advice shows another example of God’s care and guidance. This time, God did not act through a miracle. Instead, He used practical wisdom.

Jethro taught Moses a new way to lead. He told Moses to share his authority with other capable leaders. This way of leadership is called delegation. It means giving responsibility to others.

Before this, Moses seems to have followed an Egyptian style of leadership. That system was very strict and top\-down. All authority came from the pharaoh, who made decisions by his own command. No Egyptian law code from that time has been found.

Jethro suggested a different structure. By appointing leaders over groups of people, the work of Moses would become easier and more orderly.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 18:13-27

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`); Truth (ID: `keyterm:Truth`); Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Peace (ID: `keyterm:IntactState`)

## Exodus 18:17–18 (id: 11106)

## Exodus 19:10–15 (id: 11110)

* In the second stage of preparation, the people received commands that helped them get ready for God’s covenant.

    First, they had to wash their clothing ([19:10](https://ref.ly/Exod19:10), [14](https://ref.ly/Exod19:14)). This act showed that God is pure.

    Second, they had to set up a boundary around the mountain and not cross it ([19:12–13](https://ref.ly/Exod19:12-Exod19:13)). This command showed that God is holy (completely set apart).

    Third, they had to abstain from sexual relations ([19:15](https://ref.ly/Exod19:15)). This instruction reminded them that God is not a sexual being and that his blessings do not come from sexual activity.

    These actions were not sinful in themselves. Under normal conditions, they were good and natural. However, the people had to set them aside for a special purpose.

    They needed to focus completely on the Lord, their King, who was about to appear before them.

    Only their obedience to the boundary command is clearly reported ([19:23](https://ref.ly/Exod19:23)). Even so, since no judgment from the Lord is recorded, it is reasonable to conclude that they obeyed the other commands as well.

* [Hebrews 12:18–22](https://ref.ly/Heb12:18-Heb12:22) compares this event at Mount Sinai with the believer’s experience at Mount Zion, which it calls “the heavenly Jerusalem.”

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 19:10-15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Mount Sinai (ID: `place:SinaiMount`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Exodus 19:16–25 (id: 11111)

The final preparation phase included events that helped people accept the covenant. Some of the experiences were visual: lightning and a cloud ([19:16](https://ref.ly/Exod19:16)), smoke and fire ([19:18](https://ref.ly/Exod19:18)). The people also heard thunder, a ram’s horn, and the voice of God ([19:16](https://ref.ly/Exod19:16), [19](https://ref.ly/Exod19:19)). The entire mountain shook violently ([19:18](https://ref.ly/Exod19:18)). In the Bible, such unusual events are often associated with a *theophany,* which is a visible appearance of God’s holy presence.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 19:16-25

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Mount Sinai (ID: `place:SinaiMount`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Rams Horn (ID: `realia:RamsHorn`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Kiln (ID: `realia:Kiln`)

God is completely holy and far above his creation (transcendent, meaning beyond human limits). People must approach him in the way he commands. They may not come to him in their own way.

## Exodus 20:1–23.33 (id: 11137)

The Sinai covenant follows closely the pattern of treaties made in the ancient Near East. In those treaties, a great king made an agreement with smaller nations. The great king was called a *suzerain* (a powerful ruler). The smaller nations were called *vassals* (people or nations under his rule).

These treaties usually followed a clear structure:

1. The treaty began with an introduction. This section named the great king who was offering the covenant ([20:1](https://ref.ly/Exod20:1)).
2. Next came a historical preamble (an introduction that explains past events). This section described what had already happened between the king and the people ([20:2](https://ref.ly/Exod20:2)).

    In Exodus, God reminds Israel that he brought them out of slavery in Egypt. This shows why he has the right to make a covenant with them.

3. Then the treaty listed the stipulations (the required terms of the agreement). These explained what each side promised to do. In ancient treaties, the king promised protection from enemies and help in times of danger. The people promised loyalty and obedience.

    In Exodus, the basic terms of the covenant appear in [20:3–17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:3-Exod20:17). These verses include the Ten Commandments. The laws are explained further in chapters [21–23](https://ref.ly/Exod21:1-Exod23:33).

4. The treaty also explained where the written copy should be kept and when it should be read publicly ([24:7](https://ref.ly/Exod24:7); [25:16](https://ref.ly/Exod25:16)). This ensured that future generations would remember the covenant.
5. Ancient treaties called on the gods to witness the agreement. In Exodus, instead of calling on pagan gods, historical events and written records serve as witnesses ([24:4](https://ref.ly/Exod24:4)). This shows that Israel’s covenant was different from pagan treaties.
6. Finally, treaties listed blessings and curses. Blessings would follow obedience. Curses would follow disobedience ([23:20–33](https://ref.ly/Exod23:20-Exod23:33)). This made it clear that the covenant had real consequences.

God used the familiar political treaty form of the ancient world. However, he removed pagan religious elements that were common at that time.

Through this covenant, God invited Israel into a formal relationship with himself as their king.

The Sermon on the Mount in the New Testament parallels this section of Exodus ([Matthew 5:1–7:29](https://ref.ly/Matt5:1-Matt7:29)). The Beatitudes ([Matthew 5:3–12](https://ref.ly/Matt5:3-Matt5:12)) correspond in some ways to the Ten Commandments.

Both passages describe how God’s people should live under his rule.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:1-23:33

## Exodus 20:2 (id: 11150)

This sentence is part of the introduction in the ancient covenant format. It provides the historical context that makes the agreement between two groups possible and reasonable. 

We are told that the *LORD your God* offered the covenant. He was not an unknown god who offered this special relationship. He was the God of Israel’s ancestors who showed his power and care directly to them. This revelation was a gracious act of rescue, proving he is the only God.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 20:3 (id: 11153)

Not recognizing any other god is the first step toward learning the truth that there are no other gods besides the Lord (see [Deuteronomy 4:35](https://ref.ly/Deut4:35); [6:4](https://ref.ly/Deut6:4); [Isaiah 43:10–11](https://ref.ly/Isa43:10-Isa43:11); [45:21–22](https://ref.ly/Isa45:21-Isa45:22)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 20:3–11 (id: 11151)

The first four commandments focus on a person’s relationship with God. These commandments teach people how to honor and worship him in the right way.

If the Israelites obeyed these commandments, they would understand who God truly is. This would help them reject false ideas about God.

In Egypt, they had seen people worship many idols (images used as gods). Later, in Canaan, they would see similar idol worship again. The first four commandments helped them stay faithful to the one true God instead of following those false beliefs.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:3-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Jealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Pronounce Innocent (ID: `keyterm:PronounceInnocent`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); Graven Image (ID: `realia:GravenImage`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Exodus 20:3–17 (id: 11152)

The terms of the covenant (promises between God and his people) describe how the people were expected to behave if they were to be in a covenant with God. The first four instructions relate directly to God ([20:2–11](https://ref.ly/Exod20:2-Exod20:11)). The last six are about relationships between people ([20:12–17](https://ref.ly/Exod20:12-Exod20:17)). 

A covenant with God explains how we should treat one another. God is deeply concerned with what is right and wrong. He expects his people to show his character in all their relationships.

Other law codes in the ancient Near East did not include such strong and absolute commands. This may be because polytheism (belief in many gods) does not support one clear and final standard for everyone.

In contrast, covenants made with a human king often included absolute commands. A single king could demand complete obedience from his subjects.

In Israel’s covenant, the one King is the Creator of the universe. He alone has the full authority to declare what is right and wrong. Because of who he is, he can give absolute principles, commands, and prohibitions.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:3-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Jealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Pronounce Innocent (ID: `keyterm:PronounceInnocent`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Graven Image (ID: `realia:GravenImage`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`)

## Exodus 20:4 (id: 11154)

Refusing to make an image of God is the first step in recognizing that he is transcendent. This means God is the Creator of the universe and is completely distinct from it. God is not part of creation.

If people represent God as something in creation, they will eventually worship that created thing. Instead of worshiping the Creator, they begin to worship what God made.

This exchange leads to moral corruption and spiritual harm ([Romans 1:18–25](https://ref.ly/Rom1:18-Rom1:25)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:4

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Graven Image (ID: `realia:GravenImage`)

## Exodus 20:5–6 (id: 11155)

* *jealous God:* When Scripture calls God a jealous God, it means he strongly opposes his people turning to false gods. He does not want them to give their loyalty to other gods (see [Joshua 24:19–20](https://ref.ly/Josh24:19-Josh24:20)). God’s jealousy is not selfish. It shows his deep love and his desire for his people to remain faithful to him.
* *to the third and fourth generations … a thousand generations:* We must understand both parts of this statement together. God does not punish children for the sins of their parents. Instead, God teaches that sin has effects that can harm future generations.

    Even so, God limits the natural effects of sin to three or four generations. In contrast, he extends the effects of obedience to a thousand generations. This shows that God’s mercy is much greater than his judgment (see also [34:6–7](https://ref.ly/Exod34:6-Exod34:7); [Deuteronomy 7:9](https://ref.ly/Deut7:9)).

* *hate:* The Hebrew words translated “love” ([Exodus 20:6](https://ref.ly/Exod20:6)) and “hate” are broader than English words. They describe not only feelings but also choices.

    In the Bible, to *love* means "to choose someone or something and to act faithfully toward that choice." To *hate* means "to reject someone or something and to act in ways that show that rejection."

    Emotions and choices are connected. What we feel influences our choices, and our choices reveal what we truly feel.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:5-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jealous (ID: `keyterm:Zealous.2`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Appoint (ID: `keyterm:Appoint`)

## Exodus 20:7 (id: 11156)

In Hebrew thought, a person’s name is often closely connected to that person’s nature and character (see study note on 3:13–22). To misuse God's name means to make God seem unimportant or worthless. One way to do this is by swearing an oath using God's name without intending to keep it. But there are other, more serious ways to dishonor God's name (see [Numbers 20:12](https://ref.ly/Num20:12); [Amos 2:7](https://ref.ly/Amos2:7); compare [Matthew 6:9](https://ref.ly/Matt6:9)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:7
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 3:13-22 (ID: `11275@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pronounce Innocent (ID: `keyterm:PronounceInnocent`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 20:8–11 (id: 11157)

The Sabbath day is holy when dedicated to the Lord ([20:10](https://ref.ly/Exod20:10)). What belongs only to God shares his character. This does not mean the other six days are unholy. They are for regular work ([20:9](https://ref.ly/Exod20:9)). On this special day, we remember that our work does not meet our needs. God provides for us through his grace ([20:6](https://ref.ly/Exod20:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:8-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Exodus 20:11 (id: 11138)

To be in covenant with God is to do what God does. Since God rested from his work, we must also rest ([Genesis 2:1–3](https://ref.ly/Gen2:1-Gen2:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sabbath (ID: `keyterm:Sabbath`); Bless (ID: `keyterm:Bless`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Exodus 20:12 (id: 11140)

*honor your father and mother:* This means acknowledging that you rely on others. This is an important step toward humility. Arrogance might make you famous, but humility and gratitude lead to a long, fulfilling life. 

Both Jesus and the apostle Paul refer to this command (see [Matthew 15:4](https://ref.ly/Matt15:4); [Mark 7:10](https://ref.ly/Mark7:10); [Ephesians 6:1–3](https://ref.ly/Eph6:1-Eph6:3)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`)

## Exodus 20:12–17 (id: 11139)

The last six instructions focus on relationships between people. Many parts of God’s covenant teach how people should treat one another.

In Israel, right behavior toward others was closely connected to worship. Obeying God meant treating other people in ways that reflect his character.

This connection between ethics (moral behavior) and religious duty was unusual in the ancient world. Several law codes existed in the ancient Near East, and some were written before Moses. However, those laws came from human kings and were not closely connected to worship.

In contrast, the false gods of other nations were described as immoral and untrustworthy. The God of Israel is different. His people honor him by treating others as he treats them. See also [Matthew 19:17–19](https://ref.ly/Matt19:17-Matt19:19).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:12-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Bring Honor and Glory on Oneself (ID: `keyterm:BringHonorAndGloryOnOneself`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`)

## Exodus 20:13 (id: 11141)

*murder:* This does not refer to every act of killing. The Hebrew language has another word that can mean killing in a more general sense.

The command does not forbid *all* taking of human life in every situation. Instead, it forbids the intentional killing of another person for personal reasons.

This command shows that God values each person’s physical life. Jesus later expanded this teaching in [Matthew 5:21–26](https://ref.ly/Matt5:21-Matt5:26).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:13


## Exodus 20:14 (id: 11142)

*adultery:* This includes all sexual sins. Adultery clearly shows what it means to break trust or be unfaithful.

When a person commits adultery, that person seeks sexual pleasure outside of marriage and breaks trust with a spouse.

This command teaches faithfulness in marriage and purity in sexual behavior. Jesus later expanded this teaching in [Matthew 5:27–30](https://ref.ly/Matt5:27-Matt5:30).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:14


## Exodus 20:15 (id: 11143)

Stealing shows an attitude that says, “I have the right to take what I want.” It ignores whether something belongs to someone else or whether it was earned honestly.

This command teaches the opposite attitude. It teaches respect for other people and for what belongs to them.

From God’s point of view, this command shows that he values each person. God cares not only about our lives but also about the things that belong to us.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:15


## Exodus 20:16 (id: 11144)

Choosing not to tell a lie can sometimes be a private act. A person might even feel proud about being honest.

However, this command is more specific. It forbids giving false testimony against your neighbor. This refers especially to lying in court or in a public setting where someone’s reputation or life may be at risk.

Refusing to testify falsely is an unselfish act. It shows that you care more about your neighbor’s reputation and well\-being than about protecting yourself or gaining an advantage.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:16


## Exodus 20:17 (id: 11145)

*covet:* This final principle connects back to the first, as “greed, which is idolatry” ([Colossians 3:5](https://ref.ly/Col3:5)). Coveting is like worshiping the world, believing that possessions, especially those owned by others, will bring lasting happiness. Believing this betrays one's promise of complete loyalty to God, who alone meets our needs. See also [Romans 7:7](https://ref.ly/Rom7:7).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Exodus 20:18–21 (id: 11146)

The people were very conscious of their sinfulness before a holy God and begged Moses to stand between them and God. Moses pointed out that God’s goal was neither to overawe them nor to destroy them. Rather, he wanted their awe of him to keep them from sinning ([Exodus 20:20](https://ref.ly/Exod20:20)). Jesus is the ultimate mediator between God and people ([1 Timothy 2:5](https://ref.ly/1Tim2:5)).

## Exodus 20:22–26 (id: 11149)

* The Lord is beyond human understanding, as shown by his speaking to the people with a voice from heaven ([20:22](https://ref.ly/Exod20:22)).

    This had two effects on their practices:

    + They could not use silver or gold to make human\-shaped gods ([20:23](https://ref.ly/Exod20:23)).
        + They could not carve stones for an altar ([20:25](https://ref.ly/Exod20:25)). This might be to prevent them from carving stone gods. They must make the altar from earth and uncut stones ([20:24](https://ref.ly/Exod20:24), [25](https://ref.ly/Exod20:25)).
* The rule against revealing nakedness might have been to distinguish Israelite religious practices from the nature worship around them ([20:26](https://ref.ly/Exod20:26)). Sexual activity was a key part of many Canaanite ceremonies.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 20:22-26

## Exodus 21:1–11 (id: 11158)

Slavery was common in the ancient world. Sometimes, it was the only option for people who were very poor. Many slaves believed the gods had abandoned them. Because the Lord cares for those in this difficult situation, his followers must also care. 

In time, the message that Jesus Christ died for all people everywhere changed how believers viewed slavery. If Christ died for everyone, then all people have equal value before God.

This truth made slavery impossible to defend in the long term ([Galatians 3:28](https://ref.ly/Gal3:28)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:1-11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`); Hebrews (ID: `group:Hebrews`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Awl (ID: `realia:Awl`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Doorpost (ID: `realia:Doorpost`)

## Exodus 21:1–23.19 (id: 11159)

The statements in this section follow the usual case\-law pattern used in ancient Near Eastern law codes (legal collections from the lands around Israel). This pattern begins with a possible situation introduced by the word “if.” The next part explains what should happen and begins with the word “then.”

In these chapters, some laws are grouped together. However, the text does not clearly separate personal, civil, and ceremonial laws. These types of laws appear side by side.

This shows that, for God, life is not divided into separate parts. All of life is lived in relationship with him. Every action shows whether a person is living in loving obedience to God or in resistance against him.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:1-23:19

## Exodus 21:2 (id: 11167)

A man might sell himself into slavery in order to get money to pay his debts. This law states that the man was never to become the permanent property of the master.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); Hebrews (ID: `group:Hebrews`)

## Exodus 21:3 (id: 11169)

If a man and a wife went into slavery together, they were also to go free together.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:3


## Exodus 21:4–6 (id: 11170)

The master was not required to provide a slave with a wife who would then be freed with him. The slave in such a situation had to either abandon the family he began while he was a slave or become a permanent slave.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:4-6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Awl (ID: `realia:Awl`); Force to Serve (ID: `keyterm:EnticedToServe`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Love (ID: `keyterm:Love`); Doorpost (ID: `realia:Doorpost`)

## Exodus 21:7–9 (id: 11171)

A female slave was treated differently from a male slave. She may have been sold with the expectation that she would become a concubine (a secondary wife). Releasing her after seven years would not necessarily protect her.

If she were sent away, she might have to return to her father. Her father had already sold her, which suggests he did not want her. Marriage would also be unlikely for a former concubine. She might even be forced into prostitution. For this reason, it was often considered better for her to remain in her master’s household.

Sometimes a man bought a girl to become his son’s wife ([21:9](https://ref.ly/Exod21:9)). In that case, the payment was not the price of a slave. It was similar to a bride\-price (a gift given to a bride’s family at marriage). In the ancient Near East, a young man usually gave money or valuable gifts to the bride’s father (see [22:16](https://ref.ly/Exod22:16); [Genesis 24:53](https://ref.ly/Gen24:53)). A woman for whom a bride\-price had been paid was not considered a slave.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:7-9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Redeem (ID: `keyterm:Redeem`)

## Exodus 21:12–17 (id: 11161)

The crimes of murder, kidnapping, and cursing or striking one’s parents were punished by death. A person who committed murder gave up his own right to live. Human life is very precious in God’s sight.

Even when a death was accidental, it could not simply be ignored. However, the law made a difference between murder and accidental killing.

God provided places of refuge (see [Numbers 35:6–28\).](https://ref.ly/Num35:6-Num35:28) These were special towns where a person who killed someone by accident could flee. There, the family of the dead person could not take revenge (see also study notes on Numbers 35:6–34).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:12-17
* **Associated Articles:** Numbers 35:6-34 (ID: `22965@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Crafty (ID: `keyterm:Crafty`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`)

*take him away from My altar:* See [1 Kings 2:28–33](https://ref.ly/1Kgs2:28-1Kgs2:33).

These laws governed compensation for injuries that did not lead to death.

## Exodus 21:22–25 (id: 11165)

*her child is born prematurely:* It appears that if a child’s birth was caused prematurely and the child died (that is, there was further injury), the penalty for murder was to be enacted. The law of retaliation (the *lex talionis*) called for a penalty that matched the injury inflicted on a victim. But this law also served to limit the punishment so it was not more severe than the original injury. Compare [Matthew 5:38–39](https://ref.ly/Matt5:38-Matt5:39).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:22-25


## Exodus 21:28–32 (id: 11166)

If an animal caused a person’s death and the owner was judged to be negligent, the relatives of the deceased could demand the death penalty for both the animal and its owner. However, that penalty was not mandatory, and the relatives could elect to accept compensation ([21:30](https://ref.ly/Exod21:30)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:28-32

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Exempt (ID: `keyterm:Exempt`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Shekel (ID: `realia:Shekel`)

## Exodus 21:33–22.15 (id: 11168)

In cases involving loss of property, the responsible person had to pay compensation equal to the value of what was lost. If the person had actually stolen the property, the compensation was multiplied ([22:1](https://ref.ly/Exod22:1), [4](https://ref.ly/Exod22:4), [7](https://ref.ly/Exod22:7)). Where there was a question of responsibility, the parties were to appear before God for adjudication ([22:8](https://ref.ly/Exod22:8)). The means by which the judgment was reached is not reported.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 21:33-22:15

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`); Donkey (ID: `fauna:Donkey`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Money (ID: `realia:Money`); Pit (ID: `realia:Pit`); House (ID: `realia:House`); To Declare Guilty (ID: `keyterm:DeclareGuilty`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); Oath (ID: `keyterm:Oath`); Grain (ID: `flora:Grain`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`)

## Exodus 22:16–31 (id: 11172)

These laws deal with different situations about social responsibility. They are not arranged in clear groups or categories.

These laws show that every part of life relates to obedience to God. Obedience to God is not limited to worship. It includes daily actions and relationships (see study note on 21:1–23:19).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:16-31
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 21:1-23.19 (ID: `11159@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Exodus 22:18 (id: 11173)

The practices of a sorceress (a woman who uses magic or calls on spirits) reflected the pagan worldview (a way of understanding life that does not follow the true God). God was rescuing his people from this way of thinking and living.

This worldview was completely different from the biblical one. The Lord had already commanded his people not to worship other gods (see [20:3](https://ref.ly/Exod20:3); [23:13](https://ref.ly/Exod23:13)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:18

## Exodus 22:21 (id: 11175)

The Hebrews were to treat foreigners kindly because they, too, had once been foreigners (see also [23:9](https://ref.ly/Exod23:9); [Deuteronomy 10:18–19](https://ref.ly/Deut10:18-Deut10:19)). For more on this principle, see [Luke 6:31](https://ref.ly/Luke6:31).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:21

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Exodus 22:22–23 (id: 11176)

The widow*,* the orphan*,* and the foreigner were in a helpless and economically disadvantaged position. God, by his protective stance toward these persons, demonstrated that he does not value people on the basis of their power or wealth, the standards accepted by most humans. Instead, God values people because they share his ([Genesis 1:27](https://ref.ly/Gen1:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:22-23


## Exodus 22:25–27 (id: 11177)

Although interest could not be charged on a loan to another Israelite, collateral (or security) could be required. However, even the security had to be handled in a humane way. The requirements of the covenant reflected the character of God.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 22:25-27

In the Bible, blood has symbolic meaning because it represents life. For this reason, God forbade people to eat meat that still had blood in it (see [Leviticus 17:10–16](https://ref.ly/Lev17:10-Lev17:16)).

Renewal, rest, and refreshment are important ([23:12](https://ref.ly/Exod23:12)). Just as humans and animals are to enjoy these in the weekly Sabbath, so the land is to be given rest every seven years ([23:10–11](https://ref.ly/Exod23:10-Exod23:11); see study note on 20:8–11).

## Exodus 23:14–17 (id: 11183)

God commanded that every man in Israel must appear before him three times each year ([23:17](https://ref.ly/Exod23:17)). They must appear at the tabernacle (also called the *Tent of Meeting*)*.* While Israel camped around the Tent of Meeting in the wilderness, this command was not difficult to obey. The people lived close to the tabernacle.

Later, after Israel settled in the promised land, many people lived far from the Tent of Meeting and later from the temple in Jerusalem. Travel became longer and more demanding.

This command was likely meant to prevent the people from building local worship centers. If each region created its own place of worship, the nation could become divided.

Local shrines could also allow pagan influences (beliefs and practices from other religions) to enter the worship of Yahweh. By gathering at one central place, the people would remain united in their worship of the one true God.

Sadly, the people did not always carry out these commands carefully (see, for example, [2 Kings 23:21–23](https://ref.ly/2Kgs23:21-2Kgs23:23)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 23:14-17

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Unleavened Bread (ID: `realia:UnleavenedBread`)

## Exodus 23:16 (id: 11185)

* They celebrated *the* *Feast of Harvest* seven weeks after Passover. This was about the end of the barley harvest and the start of the wheat harvest (mid\-May to mid\-June). In later Judaism, this feast marked the giving of the covenant at Mount Sinai. This happened 50 days after the first Passover in Egypt (see[Leviticus 23:15–21](https://ref.ly/Lev23:15-Lev23:21); [Deuteronomy 16:9–12](https://ref.ly/Deut16:9-Deut16:12)).
* They celebrated the Feast of the Ingathering on the fifteenth day of the seventh month of the Hebrew calendar (mid\-September to mid\-October). This was after the grape harvest ended. This feast remembered the time in the wilderness when God provided for his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 23:16


## Exodus 23:18 (id: 11186)

* Blood symbolized life ([Leviticus 17:11–14](https://ref.ly/Lev17:11-Lev17:14); [Deuteronomy 12:23](https://ref.ly/Deut12:23)). So, it must not mix with anything leaven, which was usually not included in offerings (see study note on Leviticus 2:11).
* They had to immediately burn the fat. This was considered the best part of the offering (see [Leviticus 3:3–5](https://ref.ly/Lev3:3-Lev3:5)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 23:18
* **Associated Articles:** Leviticus 2:11 (ID: `20216@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`)

## Exodus 23:19 (id: 11187)

The meaning of the command not to cook a young goat in its mother’s milk is not clear.

The Bible does not explain the reason for this law. Because it appears among other worship laws, some scholars think it may have been a pagan religious practice. Pagan means related to the worship of other gods.

If that is true, the command would warn Israel not to copy the worship practices of other nations.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 23:19

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Kid (ID: `fauna:Kid`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`)

## Exodus 23:20–33 (id: 11189)

If the people obeyed the covenant rules, God promised to protect them from enemies and illness and give them land to own ([23:22–23](https://ref.ly/Exod23:22-Exod23:23),[25–26](https://ref.ly/Exod23:25-Exod23:26), [27–31](https://ref.ly/Exod23:27-Exod23:31)). These promises depended on complete loyalty to God (Yahweh). Worshiping the gods of the peoples expelled from the land would be an act of rebellion ([23:21](https://ref.ly/Exod23:21); [24](https://ref.ly/Exod23:24), [32–33](https://ref.ly/Exod23:32-Exod23:33)).

## Exodus 23:33 (id: 11194)

*they cause you to sin against Me:* God commanded Israel to destroy the Canaanites. There could be no peace agreement between a holy God and sin.

God had chosen Israel to carry his message (revelation, meaning what God makes known about himself) to the world. This message would one day lead to the coming of Jesus Christ in human form (the incarnation).

For this plan to continue, Israel had to remain faithful to God. If they returned to pagan worship (the worship of false gods), this plan would be broken.

## Exodus 24:1–18 (id: 11196)

Israel agreed to the terms of the covenant ([24:3](https://ref.ly/Exod24:3)). The covenant was then confirmed through several ceremonies:

* First, the covenant was written down and read aloud to the people ([24:4](https://ref.ly/Exod24:4), [7](https://ref.ly/Exod24:7)).
* Then blood was sprinkled as part of the ceremony ([24:6](https://ref.ly/Exod24:6)).
* The leaders also shared a covenant meal in God’s presence ([24:11](https://ref.ly/Exod24:11)).
* Finally, the glory of the Lord appeared on the mountain ([24:15–18](https://ref.ly/Exod24:15-Exod24:18)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 24:1-18

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Moses (ID: `person:Moses`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Covenant (ID: `keyterm:Covenant`); Abihu (ID: `person:Abihu`); Nadab (ID: `person:Nadab`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Hur (ID: `person:Hur.2`); Mount Sinai (ID: `place:SinaiMount`); Joshua (ID: `person:Joshua.2`); Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Slaughtering (ID: `keyterm:Slaughtering`); Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`); Brick (ID: `realia:Brick`); Tablet (ID: `realia:Tablet`); Scroll (ID: `realia:Scroll`)

## Exodus 24:4 (id: 11203)

Pillars were set up as memorial stones. They showed that something important had happened at that place and time.

In Hittite treaties, people called on their pagan gods to witness the agreement. The gods were believed to see and confirm the treaty.

However, the Hebrews were not allowed to recognize other gods. Instead of calling on false gods as witnesses, they used stone pillars to mark the agreement.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 24:4

## Exodus 25:1–27.19 (id: 11207)

These instructions first list the materials that the Israelites could give to build the tabernacle ([25:3–7](https://ref.ly/Exod25:3-Exod25:7)). 

Next, the instructions describe the tabernacle from the center and move outward. They begin with the ark of the covenant (also called the ark of the Testimony, [25:10–22](https://ref.ly/Exod25:10-Exod25:22); [27:9–19](https://ref.ly/Exod27:9-Exod27:19)). 

Two items are described later. These are the altar of incense inside the sanctuary and the bronze basin in the courtyard ([30:1–10](https://ref.ly/Exod30:1-Exod30:10), [17–21](https://ref.ly/Exod30:17-Exod30:21)). 

These items appear in the section about the priesthood. They may be placed there because the priests used them in a special way as part of their service.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:1-9

## Exodus 25:1–40.38 (id: 11208)

Unlike most churches today, the Tent of Meeting (the tabernacle) was not mainly a place where people gathered to meet. It was first of all the dwelling place of God. It functioned like the palace of a king. God was Israel’s divine King, and the Tabernacle was his royal tent.

In this way, churches today are more similar to later Jewish synagogues, where people gathered to worship and learn (see also study note on 40:2). 

The last section of Exodus completes God’s self\-revelation to Israel. Through these events, God brought the people out of the spiritual darkness that had held them in slavery.

This section includes three main parts:

1. Instructions for building the Tent of Meeting (chapters [25–31](https://ref.ly/Exod25:1-Exod31:18)).
2. The story of the golden calf (a wrong attempt to secure God's presence, chapters [32–34](https://ref.ly/Exod32:1-Exod34:35)).
3. The report of building the Tent of Meeting (the correct way to secure God's presence, chapters [35–40](https://ref.ly/Exod35:1-Exod40:38)).

These chapters show that God’s ultimate goal in delivering Israel from slavery was to live among them.

God wanted to share his presence with his people. However, because God is holy (completely pure and set apart from sin), people could experience his presence only in the way he commanded.

They could not approach him through pagan practices (worship methods of other nations) or through human manipulation (trying to control or force a god to act).

The building of the Tent of Meeting is described here, even though some instructions in Leviticus were given earlier (see [40:1–2](https://ref.ly/Exod40:1-Exod40:2); [Leviticus 25:1](https://ref.ly/Lev25:1); [27:34](https://ref.ly/Lev27:34); [Numbers 7:1](https://ref.ly/Num7:1)). This event shows the goal of the entire exodus story: God came to live among his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:1-9
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 40:2 (ID: `11388@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

* The purpose of this activity was so God could live among them.

## Exodus 25:9 (id: 11219)

*according to the pattern:* We cannot choose how to approach a holy God. We must meet God on his terms and in accord with his desires and character.

## Exodus 25:10–22 (id: 11209)

*an ark of acacia wood:* *Ark* is an old English word meaning "box." The ark was Israel's "covenant box." It was the most important item in the entire Tent of Meeting (the tabernacle) because God made his presence available there ([25:22](https://ref.ly/Exod25:22)). The stone tablets of the covenant were inside the box ([25:16](https://ref.ly/Exod25:16), [21](https://ref.ly/Exod25:21)). This emphasized that God reveals himself through a mutual commitment between himself and his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:10-22

## Exodus 25:22 (id: 11213)

*I will meet with you there:* The ark was in the innermost part of the Holy Place ([26:33](https://ref.ly/Exod26:33)). This inner room was like God's throne room (see study note on Leviticus 16:2). 

In many pagan (non\-Israelite) temples, an idol stood in that place. An idol is a physical image that people worship as a god. But the tabernacle had no idol. It had only the ark, a sacred box that showed God’s covenant faithfulness and grace (his loyal love and undeserved kindness).

God met his people in that setting. He did not meet them through an idol.

An idol shows a false belief about God. It suggests that a god is part of the created world and can be controlled by the right ritual (religious action). It assumes that people can gain blessings without caring about their own character or commitment.

But the Lord is completely separate from this world. He cannot be controlled or forced to act. He wants to bless his people. Yet his blessings are gifts of grace. People must receive them in trust and surrender to him.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:22
* **Associated Articles:** Leviticus 16:2 (ID: `20161@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Exodus 25:31–39 (id: 11217)

The lampstand of pure, hammered gold stood on the south side of the tabernacle ([26:35](https://ref.ly/Exod26:35)). It symbolized God's presence with his people. 

The lamps were flat dishes holding olive oil ([25:37](https://ref.ly/Exod25:37)). A wick extended into the oil and hung from a lip on one side of the dish. The dishes sat on upturned almond blossoms ([25:33](https://ref.ly/Exod25:33)). The priests positioned them so that all their wicks faced the room's center ([25:37](https://ref.ly/Exod25:37)). 

The total number of lamps is unclear. [Exodus 25:37](https://ref.ly/Exod25:37) mentions seven lamps, but there seem to be 22 lamp cups in [25:33–34](https://ref.ly/Exod25:33-Exod25:34).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 25:31-39

## Exodus 26:1–37 (id: 11220)

The tabernacle itself (not including the courtyard around it) was not very large. It was about 4\.6 meters (15 feet) wide and 13\.7 meters (45 feet) long.

The main structure stood on 48 vertical frames. Each frame was 4\.6 meters (15 feet) high and 68\.6 centimeters (27 inches) wide ([26:16](https://ref.ly/Exod26:16)). The frames were made of acacia wood and covered with gold ([26:15](https://ref.ly/Exod26:15), [29](https://ref.ly/Exod26:29)). 

Workers joined the frames together with horizontal crossbars. These bars formed a three\-sided rectangle with one open end ([26:26–28](https://ref.ly/Exod26:26-Exod26:28)). 

Two large linen curtains covered the structure. Each large curtain was made from five smaller curtains joined together. The two large curtains were then connected into one long covering ([26:1–6](https://ref.ly/Exod26:1-Exod26:6)). 

This linen covering stretched across the top of the frame. It hung down along the sides and the back. It covered both the walls and the roof.

Over the linen curtain, workers placed another curtain made of goat hair. It was built in the same way. This curtain was 0\.9 meters (3 feet) wider and 1\.8 meters (6 feet) longer than the linen curtain ([26:7–13](https://ref.ly/Exod26:7-Exod26:13)). Because it was larger, it hung lower on every side.

Above these two curtains were two protective layers. One layer was made from tanned ram skins. The other was made from fine leather ([26:14](https://ref.ly/Exod26:14)). These outer coverings protected the tabernacle from weather and damage.

Inside the tabernacle, another curtain divided the space into two parts. This curtain was beautifully embroidered. It hung across the width of the structure on four posts made of acacia wood ([26:31–33](https://ref.ly/Exod26:31-Exod26:33)). This created two rooms. The first room was called the Holy Place. The second room was called the Most Holy Place.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 26:1-37

## Exodus 26:2–6 (id: 11223)

The cloth was 13 meters long (42 feet) and two meters wide (six feet). The width of two meters (six feet) may have been because of the largest loom available. They joined five of these six\-foot strips, probably by sewing, to create one continuous piece of cloth 13 meters long (42 feet) and 10 meters wide (30 feet). When connected with loops and clasps, the two cloths formed a single unit measuring 13 meters (42 feet) by 20 meters (60 feet) ([26:5–6](https://ref.ly/Exod26:5-Exod26:6)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 26:2-6

## Exodus 27:2 (id: 11235)

Archaeology shows that the horns of the altar were raised parts at the four corners of the altar. They projected upward from each corner.

These horns may have helped keep the wood and offerings from falling off the altar (see [Psalm 118:27](https://ref.ly/Ps118:27)). They also had meaning related to beliefs about God. The horns represented God’s gracious care for his people.

If a person held onto the horns of the altar, that person was asking for mercy in judgment (see [1 Kings 1:50–51](https://ref.ly/1Kgs1:50-1Kgs1:51); [2:28](https://ref.ly/1Kgs2:28)). This action showed a request for protection or leniency.

The priests put blood on the horns during atonement and purification ceremonies (special rituals to deal with sin and uncleanness; see [Exodus 29:12](https://ref.ly/Exod29:12); [Leviticus 4:7](https://ref.ly/Lev4:7)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 27:2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Horns of Altar (ID: `realia:HornsOfAltar`)

## Exodus 27:20–30.38 (id: 11233)

After explaining how to build the Tent of Meeting, Moses gave instructions for those serving in it and the items used. Included are:

* the priests' duties ([27:20–21](https://ref.ly/Exod27:20-Exod27:21); [29:38–46](https://ref.ly/Exod29:38-Exod29:46)),
* the priests' clothing (chapter [28](https://ref.ly/Exod28:1-Exod28:43)),
* the ceremonies to set the priests apart for service ([29:1–37](https://ref.ly/Exod29:1-Exod29:37)),
* the tabernacle furnishings ([30:1–10](https://ref.ly/Exod30:1-Exod30:10), [17–21](https://ref.ly/Exod30:17-Exod30:21)), and
* the supplies used for worship ([30:11–16](https://ref.ly/Exod30:11-Exod30:16), [22–38](https://ref.ly/Exod30:22-Exod30:38)).

## Exodus 27:21 (id: 11234)

*the Tent of Meeting:* The expression *Tent of Meeting* sometimes refers to the tabernacle tent. In these cases, *Tent of Meeting* and *tabernacle* mean the same thing.

In addition, [33:7–11](https://ref.ly/Exod33:7-Exod33:11) also mentions a temporary tent called the T*ent of Meeting*. This tent was set up outside the camp before the tabernacle was built. See study note on 40:2.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 27:21
* **Associated Articles:** Exodus 40:2 (ID: `11388@TyndaleStudyNotes`)

## Exodus 28:1–2 (id: 11238)

The garments described in this chapter are primarily those for Aaron, the first high priest. The clothing for Aaron’s sons, who functioned as his assistants, is described in [28:40–43](https://ref.ly/Exod28:40-Exod28:43).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:1-2

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Priesthood (ID: `keyterm:Priesthood`); Abihu (ID: `person:Abihu`); Eleazar (ID: `person:Eleazar.2`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Ithamar (ID: `person:Ithamar`); Nadab (ID: `person:Nadab`)

## Exodus 28:1–43 (id: 11239)

These holy garments for Aaron were to distinguish him as a priest set apart (Hebrew *qadash*, “consecrated”) for the Lord's service ([28:2](https://ref.ly/Exod28:2), [3](https://ref.ly/Exod28:3)). The beauty of these garments matched the beauty of the Tent of Meeting, which was God's portable earthly palace (or tabernacle). 

One day, Jesus Christ, the true High Priest, would enter the Most Holy Place once for all. Then the difference between priests and people would disappear ([Hebrews 10:11–22](https://ref.ly/Heb10:11-Heb10:22)). Until then, human mediators represented how Christ would fulfill the types of wonderful things God accomplishes for his people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:1-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Breast-Piece (ID: `realia:Breast-piece`); Ephod (ID: `realia:Ephod`); Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Priesthood (ID: `keyterm:Priesthood`); Turban (ID: `realia:Turban`); Craftsman (ID: `realia:Craftsman`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`); Holy Place (ID: `place:HolyPlace`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Sash (ID: `realia:Sash`); Robe (ID: `realia:Robe`); Tunic (ID: `realia:Tunic`); Spindle (ID: `realia:Spindle`); Glory (ID: `keyterm:Glory`); Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Train (ID: `realia:Train`); Pomegranate (ID: `flora:Pomegranate`); Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Thread (ID: `realia:Thread`); Jinglet (ID: `realia:Jinglet`); Smear (ID: `keyterm:Smear`); Abihu (ID: `person:Abihu`); Eleazar (ID: `person:Eleazar.2`); Nadab (ID: `person:Nadab`); Delight (ID: `keyterm:Delight`); Ithamar (ID: `person:Ithamar`); Ability (ID: `keyterm:Ability`); Plate (ID: `realia:Plate`); Span (ID: `realia:Span`); Embroidered Cloth (ID: `realia:EmbroideredCloth`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tent of Meeting (ID: `realia:TentOfMeeting`); Breeches (ID: `realia:Breeches`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`)

## Exodus 28:6–14 (id: 11248)

*the ephod:* The ephod was a kind of apron consisting of front and back pieces joined by shoulder straps and secured with a decorative sash ([28:7](https://ref.ly/Exod28:7), [8](https://ref.ly/Exod28:8), [27–28](https://ref.ly/Exod28:27-Exod28:28)). Moses probably mentions it first because it was a unique garment of the high priest. 

Gideon later made a gold ephod. This suggests that people believed the garment had special powers ([Judges 8:27](https://ref.ly/Judg8:27)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:6-14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Ephod (ID: `realia:Ephod`); Craftsman (ID: `realia:Craftsman`); Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`); Spindle (ID: `realia:Spindle`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`)

## Exodus 28:12 (id: 11240)

*names on his two shoulders* (see [Isaiah 9:6](https://ref.ly/Isa9:6)): The badge of office on the shoulder indicated what the person was really carrying. Thus, the high priest represented the people before God, and with his royal garments he represented the glory of God before the people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:12

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Ephod (ID: `realia:Ephod`)

## Exodus 28:15–30 (id: 11241)

The *breastpiece* receives more attention than any other item. This suggests the breastpiece was very important. 

The breastpiece symbolized the priest's representation of Israel ([28:17–21](https://ref.ly/Exod28:17-Exod28:21), [29](https://ref.ly/Exod28:29)). It held *the Urim and Thummim*, objects through which God revealed his will ([28:30](https://ref.ly/Exod28:30)). 

The high priest had two main tasks. First, he brought the people to God. He represented them before the Lord.

Second, he brought God’s word to the people. He spoke to them on God’s behalf.

The chestpiece stood for both of these duties. It showed that the high priest served as a bridge between God and the people.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:15-30

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Breast-Piece (ID: `realia:Breast-piece`); Ephod (ID: `realia:Ephod`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Rope (ID: `realia:Rope`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Purple Cloth (ID: `realia:PurpleCloth`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Pure (ID: `keyterm:Pure`); Holy Place (ID: `place:HolyPlace`); Span (ID: `realia:Span`); Spindle (ID: `realia:Spindle`); Craftsman (ID: `realia:Craftsman`); Measuring Reed (ID: `realia:MeasuringReed`); Thread (ID: `realia:Thread`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`)

## Exodus 28:30 (id: 11243)

*the Urim and Thummim:* These are Hebrew terms that might translate as *lights* \[or *curses*] *and perfections.* These objects were likely two stones. The priest used them to receive a yes\-or\-no answer from God.

The Bible does not describe what these stones looked like. Other passages further explain their use (see [Numbers 27:21](https://ref.ly/Num27:21); [Deuteronomy 33:8](https://ref.ly/Deut33:8); [1 Samuel 28:6](https://ref.ly/1Sam28:6); [Ezra 2:63](https://ref.ly/Ezra2:63); and [Nehemiah 7:65](https://ref.ly/Neh7:65)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:30

## Exodus 28:31–35 (id: 11244)

*the robe of the ephod:* The bells signaled that the priest was still moving and had not been struck dead by God's presence (see [Leviticus 10:1–2](https://ref.ly/Lev10:1-Lev10:2)). Jewish tradition says that the priest had a cord tied to his ankle. If he died in the Holy Place, others could drag his body out ([Exodus 28:35](https://ref.ly/Exod28:35)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:31-35

## Exodus 28:36–38 (id: 11245)

Like all other parts of the high priest's clothing and actions, the little gold plate on the front of his turban reminded the people that God is holy. His holiness affected how they could enter his presence. God wanted to live with his people, but his holiness would harm them unless he took protective measures. God decides how we can enter his presence, not us. 

The Bible's main question is how sinful people can live with a holy God so that God can share his holy character with them. Jesus Christ, the Holy One of God, provides the answer. He is the perfect mediator ([Luke 4:34](https://ref.ly/Luke4:34); [Hebrews 9:11–15](https://ref.ly/Heb9:11-Heb9:15)). He did not die only to save us from the consequences of our sins. He died so that God can live in us and create his holy character in us ([Ephesians 2:10](https://ref.ly/Eph2:10); [4:22](https://ref.ly/Eph4:22); [Philippians 1:27](https://ref.ly/Phil1:27); [1 Peter 1:13–16](https://ref.ly/1Pet1:13-1Pet1:16)).

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:36-38

## Exodus 28:42–43 (id: 11247)

In many pagan religions, sexual acts were part of worship. People believed they could control fertility (the ability to have children, crops, and animals) through sexual activity.

In some places, priests even served their gods without clothing. These practices were attempts to influence the powers of nature.

In the worship of the Lord, no form of sexual manipulation was allowed. God’s blessings cannot be gained through magical actions or human control.

For this reason, even the accidental exposure of the genitals was carefully avoided. Worship of the Lord had to reflect his holiness and purity.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 28:42-43

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Holy (ID: `keyterm:Holy`); Bear the Consequences Of (ID: `keyterm:BearTheConsequencesOf`); Holy Place (ID: `place:HolyPlace`); Aaron (ID: `person:Aaron`); Breeches (ID: `realia:Breeches`); Stone Altar (ID: `realia:StoneAltar`); Tabernacle Altar (ID: `realia:TabernacleAltar`); Altar (ID: `realia:Altar`); Tent of Meeting (ID: `realia:TentOfMeeting`); Temple Altar (ID: `realia:TempleAltar`); Linen (ID: `realia:Linen`)

## Exodus 29:1 (id: 11256)

*without blemish:* This phrase uses the same Hebrew word that is translated *blameless* and *perfect* when referring to human and divine behavior (for example, [Genesis 17:1](https://ref.ly/Gen17:1); [Psalm 18:30](https://ref.ly/Ps18:30), [32](https://ref.ly/Ps18:32)). 

The animal offered as a sacrifice had to be without physical flaws. This showed that the sacrifice represented God’s own character. It also showed the kind of character God desires in human beings.

* **Associated Passages:** Exodus 29:1
